I guess I’m here because things have gotten… bigger than I can keep up with. Yes… anxious is probably the right word, though it feels more like this constant pressure, like everything is stacked up behind me and I’m trying to keep it from falling over. I came in because I’ve taken on way more than I can realistically manage — projects, workshops, studio changes I thought would “improve the flow,” client calls at odd hours, all of it.

At the time each thing felt important, and honestly manageable if I just organized it correctly, but now the deadlines are closing in and I’m realizing I may have stretched myself too far. And then the mortgage is there in the background, so it’s not just creative stress — it feels like if I don’t keep everything moving, something important could slip. So yes, I’m worried about not meeting the deadlines, but also about the whole workload getting away from me.

I’m not sure I’d call them repetitive thoughts exactly. I mean, I do go over things in my head — the schedule, what I promised people, whether I’ve forgotten some detail — but it feels more like trying to keep track of too many moving pieces than one particular thought looping in a way I can’t stop.

I don’t think so, no… not in the way I think you mean. I don’t have, like, a set thing I have to do a certain number of times or a ritual I believe will make the anxiety go away.

I do sometimes rearrange things in the studio or make lists and then rewrite the lists, but that feels more like trying to get a handle on the chaos than a compulsion. Like if the brushes are in the right place or the schedule is clearer, maybe I can breathe a little and see what needs to happen next. But I’m not thinking, “If I don’t do this exact thing, something terrible will happen.” It’s more practical, or at least it seems practical at the time.

So… I guess I’d say I use organizing to calm myself down, but I haven’t noticed any specific rituals that I feel forced to do.

I keep thinking I can make it all work if I just push harder, but realistically I’ve taken on more than one person can manage. So… I thought I should come in before it gets completely out of hand.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling anxious about not meeting these deadlines or managing your workload?\n\nPatient: Yes… anxious is probably the right word, though it feels more like this constant pressure, like everything is stacked up behind me and I’m trying to keep it from falling over. I came in because I’ve taken on way more than I can realistically manage — projects, workshops, studio changes I thought would “improve the flow,” client calls at odd hours, all of it.\n\nAt the time each thing felt important, and honestly manageable if I just organized it correctly, but now the deadlines are closing in and I’m realizing I may have stretched myself too far. And then the mortgage is there in the background, so it’s not just creative stress — it feels like if I don’t keep everything moving, something important could slip. So yes, I’m worried about not meeting the deadlines, but also about the whole workload getting away from me.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself having repetitive thoughts that you can't seem to stop thinking about?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call them repetitive thoughts exactly. I mean, I do go over things in my head — the schedule, what I promised people, whether I’ve forgotten some detail — but it feels more like trying to keep track of too many moving pieces than one particular thought looping in a way I can’t stop.\n\nIf you mean like… the same phrase or fear coming back over and over no matter what I do, I don’t think I’ve noticed that. It’s more that my mind keeps returning to the whole pile of responsibilities because there are so many of them, and I’m worried I’ll miss something important.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding certain tasks or situations because of feeling overwhelmed?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say I’m avoiding things in a deliberate way. It’s more… I’ll look at the list and some parts of it feel so tangled that I don’t know where to start, so I end up doing something nearby instead — reorganizing supplies, answering a less important email, checking the calendar again — things that still feel like work, but maybe aren’t the thing that actually needs doing.\n\nBut I don’t know if that counts as avoidance or just being overloaded. I haven’t really been saying, “I can’t face this, so I won’t do it.” It’s more like I circle around the harder tasks because there are so many steps and I’m trying to find the right entry point. Then suddenly an hour has gone by and I’ve handled ten little things, but not the main thing I meant to start.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel compelled to perform specific rituals or behaviors to reduce your anxiety?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, no… not in the way I think you mean. I don’t have, like, a set thing I have to do a certain number of times or a ritual I believe will make the anxiety go away.\n\nI do sometimes rearrange things in the studio or make lists and then rewrite the lists, but that feels more like trying to get a handle on the chaos than a compulsion. Like if the brushes are in the right place or the schedule is clearer, maybe I can breathe a little and see what needs to happen next. But I’m not thinking, “If I don’t do this exact thing, something terrible will happen.” It’s more practical, or at least it seems practical at the time.\n\nSo… I guess I’d say I use organizing to calm myself down, but I haven’t noticed any specific rituals that I feel forced to do.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

It’s not just work, unfortunately. I mean, work is where it shows up most visibly because there are deadlines and other people can see when something slips. My illustration deadlines have slipped more than once, and a couple of colleagues have had to check in with me, not in an accusatory way exactly, but more like, “Are you still on track?” or “Do you need help figuring out the next step?” And that feels awful, because I’m used to being the person who can handle a lot.

But it’s spilled over beyond that. The bigger thing — and I’m embarrassed to say this — is that I overcommitted. I said yes to too many projects, too many plans, too many purchases for the studio, thinking I could make it all work if I just pushed harder. And I spent money that I really needed to keep the studio secure. So now the mortgage is at risk, which is… that’s not just a work deadline problem. That’s my home, my stability, everything.

So yes, the pressure started around work and deadlines, or at least that’s where I noticed it first, but it’s affecting the rest of my life now. It’s gotten into finances and housing and my relationships with colleagues who are having to watch me more closely than they should have to.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t know if I’d put it that strongly. I haven’t noticed myself completely pulling away from people or not liking things I usually like. It’s more that when I’m with someone, part of my mind is still sort of… hovering over the unfinished things. So I might be sitting with a friend or talking to family, and I’m trying to be present, but there’s this background noise of, “You should be handling that other thing.”

But I wouldn’t say I can’t enjoy them at all. If someone gets me out of my own head a little, I can still laugh, I can still get absorbed in a conversation, and I still like being around people I care about. Same with activities — I can still appreciate them once I’m actually there. It’s just harder to settle into them because the pressure keeps tugging at me.

So… maybe it interferes some, but I haven’t really been avoiding friends or family because of it, at least not in a clear way that I’ve noticed.

It’s more that my mind keeps returning to the whole pile of responsibilities because there are so many of them, and I’m worried I’ll miss something important.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding certain tasks or situations because of feeling overwhelmed?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say I’m avoiding things in a deliberate way. It’s more… I’ll look at the list and some parts of it feel so tangled that I don’t know where to start, so I end up doing something nearby instead — reorganizing supplies, answering a less important email, checking the calendar again — things that still feel like work, but maybe aren’t the thing that actually needs doing.\n\nBut I don’t know if that counts as avoidance or just being overloaded. I haven’t really been saying, “I can’t face this, so I won’t do it.” It’s more like I circle around the harder tasks because there are so many steps and I’m trying to find the right entry point. Then suddenly an hour has gone by and I’ve handled ten little things, but not the main thing I meant to start.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel compelled to perform specific rituals or behaviors to reduce your anxiety?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, no… not in the way I think you mean. I don’t have, like, a set thing I have to do a certain number of times or a ritual I believe will make the anxiety go away.\n\nI do sometimes rearrange things in the studio or make lists and then rewrite the lists, but that feels more like trying to get a handle on the chaos than a compulsion. Like if the brushes are in the right place or the schedule is clearer, maybe I can breathe a little and see what needs to happen next. I haven’t noticed myself completely pulling away from people or not liking things I usually like. It’s more that when I’m with someone, part of my mind is still sort of… hovering over the unfinished things. So I might be sitting with a friend or talking to family, and I’m trying to be present, but there’s this background noise of, “You should be handling that other thing.”\n\nBut I wouldn’t say I can’t enjoy them at all. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… there is one thing, and I know it’s going to sound a little extreme when I say it out loud. It wasn’t exactly a normal “I’m worried they won’t like my work” kind of worry. I became really certain that a couple of clients — and even one editor I’ve worked with — were trying to get access to my sketches before I unveiled the whole system I’d been developing.

I kept thinking they were asking for updates or files in a way that wasn’t just about the project. Like there was some other motive underneath it. And people tried to reassure me, they’d say, “No, we’re just checking in,” or “We need this for scheduling,” things like that, but it didn’t really settle me. I would hear it and understand what they were saying, but the certainty didn’t go away.

It’s uncomfortable to admit because part of me can hear how suspicious it sounds. But at the time it felt very real, like I had to protect the sketches until I was ready to reveal everything properly.

That’s my home, my stability, everything.\n\nSo yes, the pressure started around work and deadlines, or at least that’s where I noticed it first, but it’s affecting the rest of my life now. It’s gotten into finances and housing and my relationships with colleagues who are having to watch me more closely than they should have to.\n\nDoctor: Are you finding it difficult to enjoy time with friends and family or engage in activities you normally like to do because of the pressure and anxiety you're feeling?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d put it that strongly. I haven’t noticed myself completely pulling away from people or not liking things I usually like. It’s more that when I’m with someone, part of my mind is still sort of… hovering over the unfinished things. So I might be sitting with a friend or talking to family, and I’m trying to be present, but there’s this background noise of, “You should be handling that other thing.”\n\nBut I wouldn’t say I can’t enjoy them at all. If someone gets me out of my own head a little, I can still laugh, I can still get absorbed in a conversation, and I still like being around people I care about. Same with activities — I can still appreciate them once I’m actually there. It’s just harder to settle into them because the pressure keeps tugging at me.\n\nSo… maybe it interferes some, but I haven’t really been avoiding friends or family because of it, at least not in a clear way that I’ve noticed.\n\nDoctor: Is there anything in particular that you worry about when you're not thinking about work or deadlines?\n\nPatient: Yes… there is one thing, and I know it’s going to sound a little extreme when I say it out loud. It wasn’t exactly a normal “I’m worried they won’t like my work” kind of worry. I became really certain that a couple of clients — and even one editor I’ve worked with — were trying to get access to my sketches before I unveiled the whole system I’d been developing.\n\nI kept thinking they were asking for updates or files in a way that wasn’t just about the project. Like there was some other motive underneath it. And people tried to reassure me, they’d say, “No, we’re just checking in,” or “We need this for scheduling,” things like that, but it didn’t really settle me. I would hear it and understand what they were saying, but the certainty didn’t go away.\n\nIt’s uncomfortable to admit because part of me can hear how suspicious it sounds. But at the time it felt very real, like I had to protect the sketches until I was ready to reveal everything properly.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. Yes… though I keep hesitating over the word “irrational,” because at the time it didn’t feel irrational. It felt like I was seeing something other people weren’t taking seriously enough.

The main thing was with my sketches. I became really convinced that a couple of clients, and even an editor I’ve worked with, were trying to get access to them before I was ready to unveil the larger system I’d been developing. Like when they asked for updates or wanted files, I didn’t hear it as just normal project management. I kept thinking, “No, they’re trying to get the framework before it’s complete,” or “They want to see the underlying structure so they can use it before I present it properly.”

And people did reassure me. They would say, “We’re not trying to take anything, we just need to see where the work is,” or “This is just for scheduling.” But it didn’t settle in. I could hear the words, and a part of me could understand that maybe it sounded extreme, but the certainty stayed there anyway. It was hard to let go of, even when I tried to talk myself down.

So… I thought I should come in before it gets completely out of hand.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling anxious about not meeting these deadlines or managing your workload?\n\nPatient: Yes… anxious is probably the right word, though it feels more like this constant pressure, like everything is stacked up behind me and I’m trying to keep it from falling over. I came in because I’ve taken on way more than I can realistically manage — projects, workshops, studio changes I thought would “improve the flow,” client calls at odd hours, all of it.\n\nAt the time each thing felt important, and honestly manageable if I just organized it correctly, but now the deadlines are closing in and I’m realizing I may have stretched myself too far. And then the mortgage is there in the background, so it’s not just creative stress — it feels like if I don’t keep everything moving, something important could slip. So yes, I’m worried about not meeting the deadlines, but also about the whole workload getting away from me.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself having repetitive thoughts that you can't seem to stop thinking about?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call them repetitive thoughts exactly. I mean, I do go over things in my head — the schedule, what I promised people, whether I’ve forgotten some detail — but it feels more like trying to keep track of too many moving pieces than one particular thought looping in a way I can’t stop.\n\nIf you mean like… the same phrase or fear coming back over and over no matter what I do, I don’t think I’ve noticed that. It’s more that my mind keeps returning to the whole pile of responsibilities because there are so many of them, and I’m worried I’ll miss something important.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding certain tasks or situations because of feeling overwhelmed?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say I’m avoiding things in a deliberate way. It’s more… I’ll look at the list and some parts of it feel so tangled that I don’t know where to start, so I end up doing something nearby instead — reorganizing supplies, answering a less important email, checking the calendar again — things that still feel like work, but maybe aren’t the thing that actually needs doing.\n\nBut I don’t know if that counts as avoidance or just being overloaded. I haven’t really been saying, “I can’t face this, so I won’t do it.” It’s more like I circle around the harder tasks because there are so many steps and I’m trying to find the right entry point. Then suddenly an hour has gone by and I’ve handled ten little things, but not the main thing I meant to start.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel compelled to perform specific rituals or behaviors to reduce your anxiety?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, no… not in the way I think you mean. I don’t have, like, a set thing I have to do a certain number of times or a ritual I believe will make the anxiety go away.\n\nI do sometimes rearrange things in the studio or make lists and then rewrite the lists, but that feels more like trying to get a handle on the chaos than a compulsion. Like if the brushes are in the right place or the schedule is clearer, maybe I can breathe a little and see what needs to happen next. But I’m not thinking, “If I don’t do this exact thing, something terrible will happen.” It’s more practical, or at least it seems practical at the time.\n\nSo… I guess I’d say I use organizing to calm myself down, but I haven’t noticed any specific rituals that I feel forced to do.\n\nDoctor: Is the feeling of pressure and anxiety you've described limited to your work and deadlines, or does it also affect other areas of your life?\n\nPatient: It’s not just work, unfortunately. I mean, work is where it shows up most visibly because there are deadlines and other people can see when something slips. My illustration deadlines have slipped more than once, and a couple of colleagues have had to check in with me, not in an accusatory way exactly, but more like, “Are you still on track?” or “Do you need help figuring out the next step?” And that feels awful, because I’m used to being the person who can handle a lot.\n\nBut it’s spilled over beyond that. The bigger thing — and I’m embarrassed to say this — is that I overcommitted. I said yes to too many projects, too many plans, too many purchases for the studio, thinking I could make it all work if I just pushed harder. And I spent money that I really needed to keep the studio secure. So now the mortgage is at risk, which is… that’s not just a work deadline problem. That’s my home, my stability, everything.\n\nSo yes, the pressure started around work and deadlines, or at least that’s where I noticed it first, but it’s affecting the rest of my life now. It’s gotten into finances and housing and my relationships with colleagues who are having to watch me more closely than they should have to.\n\nDoctor: Are you finding it difficult to enjoy time with friends and family or engage in activities you normally like to do because of the pressure and anxiety you're feeling?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d put it that strongly. I haven’t noticed myself completely pulling away from people or not liking things I usually like. It’s more that when I’m with someone, part of my mind is still sort of… hovering over the unfinished things. So I might be sitting with a friend or talking to family, and I’m trying to be present, but there’s this background noise of, “You should be handling that other thing.”\n\nBut I wouldn’t say I can’t enjoy them at all. If someone gets me out of my own head a little, I can still laugh, I can still get absorbed in a conversation, and I still like being around people I care about. Same with activities — I can still appreciate them once I’m actually there. It’s just harder to settle into them because the pressure keeps tugging at me.\n\nSo… maybe it interferes some, but I haven’t really been avoiding friends or family because of it, at least not in a clear way that I’ve noticed.\n\nDoctor: Is there anything in particular that you worry about when you're not thinking about work or deadlines?\n\nPatient: Yes… there is one thing, and I know it’s going to sound a little extreme when I say it out loud. It wasn’t exactly a normal “I’m worried they won’t like my work” kind of worry. I became really certain that a couple of clients — and even one editor I’ve worked with — were trying to get access to my sketches before I unveiled the whole system I’d been developing.\n\nI kept thinking they were asking for updates or files in a way that wasn’t just about the project. Like there was some other motive underneath it. And people tried to reassure me, they’d say, “No, we’re just checking in,” or “We need this for scheduling,” things like that, but it didn’t really settle me. I would hear it and understand what they were saying, but the certainty didn’t go away.\n\nIt’s uncomfortable to admit because part of me can hear how suspicious it sounds. But at the time it felt very real, like I had to protect the sketches until I was ready to reveal everything properly.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself having thoughts that you consider unreasonable or irrational, but that you cannot stop thinking about?\n\nPatient: Yes… though I keep hesitating over the word “irrational,” because at the time it didn’t feel irrational. It felt like I was seeing something other people weren’t taking seriously enough.\n\nThe main thing was with my sketches. I became really convinced that a couple of clients, and even an editor I’ve worked with, were trying to get access to them before I was ready to unveil the larger system I’d been developing. Like when they asked for updates or wanted files, I didn’t hear it as just normal project management. I kept thinking, “No, they’re trying to get the framework before it’s complete,” or “They want to see the underlying structure so they can use it before I present it properly.”\n\nAnd people did reassure me. They would say, “We’re not trying to take anything, we just need to see where the work is,” or “This is just for scheduling.” But it didn’t settle in. I could hear the words, and a part of me could understand that maybe it sounded extreme, but the certainty stayed there anyway. It was hard to let go of, even when I tried to talk myself down.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [doctor] ==========
{"candidates": ["F43.0", "F41.0", "F42.2"], "note": "", "is_final": false}

Like when they asked for updates or wanted files, I didn’t hear it as just normal project management. I kept thinking, “No, they’re trying to get the framework before it’s complete,” or “They want to see the underlying structure so they can use it before I present it properly.”\n\nAnd people did reassure me. They would say, “We’re not trying to take anything, we just need to see where the work is,” or “This is just for scheduling.” But it didn’t settle in. I could hear the words, and a part of me could understand that maybe it sounded extreme, but the certainty stayed there anyway. It was hard to let go of, even when I tried to talk myself down.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you feel like these worries about your clients and editor are thoughts that pop into your head without you wanting them to?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 54 / artist_or_related_worker / graduate\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Delusions\nmanifestation: \"I know it sounds extreme, but I became certain a couple of clients and even one editor were trying to get access to my sketches before I unveiled the system, and no amount of reassurance from them really settled me.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Not exactly like they “pop in” out of nowhere, no. It wasn’t like a random unwanted thought flashing through my mind and then I’d think, “Why am I thinking that?” It felt more… connected to what was happening. Like they would email asking for an update, or someone would mention needing to see the sketches, and then this whole certainty would come over me that they were trying to get access before I was ready to unveil the full system.

I know that probably sounds extreme, and I can hear that. But at the time it didn’t feel like just a nervous thought I could dismiss. It felt solid. I was certain a couple of clients, and even one editor, had some motive beyond what they were saying. And even when they reassured me — like, “No, we just need to see the progress,” or “This is normal scheduling stuff” — it didn’t really calm me down. I heard them, but it didn’t settle the feeling.

So… I wouldn’t say I wanted to be thinking about it, because it was stressful and made everything harder. But it also didn’t feel like some intrusive nonsense thought. It felt real, like I had to protect the sketches until I could reveal everything properly.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I guess I’m here because things have gotten… bigger than I can keep up with. I’m an artist, so I’m used to juggling projects, but lately I’ve been saying yes to everything — new pieces, workshops, changing things around in the studio, taking client calls at all hours, planning improvements I probably should have waited on. At first it felt productive, like I was finally getting momentum, but now I look at the list and it’s just too much.\n\nThe deadlines are starting to pile up, and there’s money pressure too — the mortgage especially is sitting in the back of my mind. I keep thinking I can make it all work if I just push harder, but realistically I’ve taken on more than one person can manage. So… I thought I should come in before it gets completely out of hand.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling anxious about not meeting these deadlines or managing your workload?\n\nPatient: Yes… anxious is probably the right word, though it feels more like this constant pressure, like everything is stacked up behind me and I’m trying to keep it from falling over. I came in because I’ve taken on way more than I can realistically manage — projects, workshops, studio changes I thought would “improve the flow,” client calls at odd hours, all of it.\n\nAt the time each thing felt important, and honestly manageable if I just organized it correctly, but now the deadlines are closing in and I’m realizing I may have stretched myself too far. And then the mortgage is there in the background, so it’s not just creative stress — it feels like if I don’t keep everything moving, something important could slip. So yes, I’m worried about not meeting the deadlines, but also about the whole workload getting away from me.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself having repetitive thoughts that you can't seem to stop thinking about?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call them repetitive thoughts exactly. I mean, I do go over things in my head — the schedule, what I promised people, whether I’ve forgotten some detail — but it feels more like trying to keep track of too many moving pieces than one particular thought looping in a way I can’t stop.\n\nIf you mean like… the same phrase or fear coming back over and over no matter what I do, I don’t think I’ve noticed that. It’s more that my mind keeps returning to the whole pile of responsibilities because there are so many of them, and I’m worried I’ll miss something important.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding certain tasks or situations because of feeling overwhelmed?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say I’m avoiding things in a deliberate way. It’s more… I’ll look at the list and some parts of it feel so tangled that I don’t know where to start, so I end up doing something nearby instead — reorganizing supplies, answering a less important email, checking the calendar again — things that still feel like work, but maybe aren’t the thing that actually needs doing.\n\nBut I don’t know if that counts as avoidance or just being overloaded. I haven’t really been saying, “I can’t face this, so I won’t do it.” It’s more like I circle around the harder tasks because there are so many steps and I’m trying to find the right entry point. Then suddenly an hour has gone by and I’ve handled ten little things, but not the main thing I meant to start.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel compelled to perform specific rituals or behaviors to reduce your anxiety?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, no… not in the way I think you mean. I don’t have, like, a set thing I have to do a certain number of times or a ritual I believe will make the anxiety go away.\n\nI do sometimes rearrange things in the studio or make lists and then rewrite the lists, but that feels more like trying to get a handle on the chaos than a compulsion. Like if the brushes are in the right place or the schedule is clearer, maybe I can breathe a little and see what needs to happen next. But I’m not thinking, “If I don’t do this exact thing, something terrible will happen.” It’s more practical, or at least it seems practical at the time.\n\nSo… I guess I’d say I use organizing to calm myself down, but I haven’t noticed any specific rituals that I feel forced to do.\n\nDoctor: Is the feeling of pressure and anxiety you've described limited to your work and deadlines, or does it also affect other areas of your life?\n\nPatient: It’s not just work, unfortunately. I mean, work is where it shows up most visibly because there are deadlines and other people can see when something slips. My illustration deadlines have slipped more than once, and a couple of colleagues have had to check in with me, not in an accusatory way exactly, but more like, “Are you still on track?” or “Do you need help figuring out the next step?” And that feels awful, because I’m used to being the person who can handle a lot.\n\nBut it’s spilled over beyond that. The bigger thing — and I’m embarrassed to say this — is that I overcommitted. I said yes to too many projects, too many plans, too many purchases for the studio, thinking I could make it all work if I just pushed harder. And I spent money that I really needed to keep the studio secure. So now the mortgage is at risk, which is… that’s not just a work deadline problem. That’s my home, my stability, everything.\n\nSo yes, the pressure started around work and deadlines, or at least that’s where I noticed it first, but it’s affecting the rest of my life now. It’s gotten into finances and housing and my relationships with colleagues who are having to watch me more closely than they should have to.\n\nDoctor: Are you finding it difficult to enjoy time with friends and family or engage in activities you normally like to do because of the pressure and anxiety you're feeling?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d put it that strongly. I haven’t noticed myself completely pulling away from people or not liking things I usually like. It’s more that when I’m with someone, part of my mind is still sort of… hovering over the unfinished things. So I might be sitting with a friend or talking to family, and I’m trying to be present, but there’s this background noise of, “You should be handling that other thing.”\n\nBut I wouldn’t say I can’t enjoy them at all. If someone gets me out of my own head a little, I can still laugh, I can still get absorbed in a conversation, and I still like being around people I care about. Same with activities — I can still appreciate them once I’m actually there. It’s just harder to settle into them because the pressure keeps tugging at me.\n\nSo… maybe it interferes some, but I haven’t really been avoiding friends or family because of it, at least not in a clear way that I’ve noticed.\n\nDoctor: Is there anything in particular that you worry about when you're not thinking about work or deadlines?\n\nPatient: Yes… there is one thing, and I know it’s going to sound a little extreme when I say it out loud. I became really certain that a couple of clients — and even one editor I’ve worked with — were trying to get access to my sketches before I unveiled the whole system I’d been developing.\n\nI kept thinking they were asking for updates or files in a way that wasn’t just about the project. I haven’t had voices or visions outside of this period.\n- key: functional_impairment\n  name: Functional Impairment\n  manifestation: My illustration deadlines have slipped, colleagues have had to check in on me, and the mortgage is now at risk because I overcommitted and spent money I needed to keep the studio secure.\n- key: duration\n  name: Duration\n  description: 3 months\n  manifestation: This has been going on for about three months, since I took on the mortgage for the cottage-studio.\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 54 / artist_or_related_worker / graduate\n- chief_complaint_symptom_code: S040\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Is there anything in particular that you worry about when you're not thinking about work or deadlines?\n\nPatient: Yes… there is one thing, and I know it’s going to sound a little extreme when I say it out loud. It wasn’t exactly a normal “I’m worried they won’t like my work” kind of worry. I became really certain that a couple of clients — and even one editor I’ve worked with — were trying to get access to my sketches before I unveiled the whole system I’d been developing.\n\nI kept thinking they were asking for updates or files in a way that wasn’t just about the project. Like there was some other motive underneath it. And people tried to reassure me, they’d say, “No, we’re just checking in,” or “We need this for scheduling,” things like that, but it didn’t really settle me. I would hear it and understand what they were saying, but the certainty didn’t go away.\n\nIt’s uncomfortable to admit because part of me can hear how suspicious it sounds. But at the time it felt very real, like I had to protect the sketches until I was ready to reveal everything properly.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself having thoughts that you consider unreasonable or irrational, but that you cannot stop thinking about?\n\nPatient: Yes… though I keep hesitating over the word “irrational,” because at the time it didn’t feel irrational. It felt like I was seeing something other people weren’t taking seriously enough.\n\nThe main thing was with my sketches. I became really convinced that a couple of clients, and even an editor I’ve worked with, were trying to get access to them before I was ready to unveil the larger system I’d been developing. Like when they asked for updates or wanted files, I didn’t hear it as just normal project management. I kept thinking, “No, they’re trying to get the framework before it’s complete,” or “They want to see the underlying structure so they can use it before I present it properly.”\n\nAnd people did reassure me. They would say, “We’re not trying to take anything, we just need to see where the work is,” or “This is just for scheduling.” But it didn’t settle in. I could hear the words, and a part of me could understand that maybe it sounded extreme, but the certainty stayed there anyway. It was hard to let go of, even when I tried to talk myself down.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these worries about your clients and editor are thoughts that pop into your head without you wanting them to?\n\nPatient: Not exactly like they “pop in” out of nowhere, no. It wasn’t like a random unwanted thought flashing through my mind and then I’d think, “Why am I thinking that?” It felt more… connected to what was happening. Like they would email asking for an update, or someone would mention needing to see the sketches, and then this whole certainty would come over me that they were trying to get access before I was ready to unveil the full system.\n\nI know that probably sounds extreme, and I can hear that. But at the time it didn’t feel like just a nervous thought I could dismiss. It felt solid. I was certain a couple of clients, and even one editor, had some motive beyond what they were saying. And even when they reassured me — like, “No, we just need to see the progress,” or “This is normal scheduling stuff” — it didn’t really calm me down. I heard them, but it didn’t settle the feeling.\n\nSo… I wouldn’t say I wanted to be thinking about it, because it was stressful and made everything harder. But it also didn’t feel like some intrusive nonsense thought. It felt real, like I had to protect the sketches until I could reveal everything properly.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you still believe that those clients and the editor were trying to get access to your sketches for reasons other than what they stated?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. If the question touches several topics, return the one it is\n  most directly about — never more than one.\n- Copy `key`, `name` and `description` exactly as they appear in [Symptom Profiles].\n- If your profile does not cover what the doctor asked, return the text \"None\" for `key`,\n  `name` and `description`, and say why in `reason`. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I… yes, mostly. I know sitting here that it sounds extreme, and I can say that out loud now in a way I maybe couldn’t before. But I did become really certain that a couple of clients and even that one editor weren’t just asking for ordinary progress updates. It felt like they were trying to get access to the sketches before I had unveiled the whole system, before I had it arranged and presented the way it needed to be.

And the thing is, people did reassure me. They said it was just scheduling, or they just needed to see where the work stood, or that they weren’t trying to take anything. But it didn’t really land. I heard them, I understood the sentences, but it didn’t settle me. There was still this feeling that there was another reason underneath what they were saying.

So… yes, I still have trouble fully letting go of the idea that there was more to it than they admitted. I know how that sounds. But at the time it felt very real, like I had to protect the sketches until I was ready.

It’s more that my mind keeps returning to the whole pile of responsibilities because there are so many of them, and I’m worried I’ll miss something important.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding certain tasks or situations because of feeling overwhelmed?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say I’m avoiding things in a deliberate way. It’s more… I’ll look at the list and some parts of it feel so tangled that I don’t know where to start, so I end up doing something nearby instead — reorganizing supplies, answering a less important email, checking the calendar again — things that still feel like work, but maybe aren’t the thing that actually needs doing.\n\nBut I don’t know if that counts as avoidance or just being overloaded. I haven’t really been saying, “I can’t face this, so I won’t do it.” It’s more like I circle around the harder tasks because there are so many steps and I’m trying to find the right entry point. Then suddenly an hour has gone by and I’ve handled ten little things, but not the main thing I meant to start.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel compelled to perform specific rituals or behaviors to reduce your anxiety?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, no… not in the way I think you mean. I don’t have, like, a set thing I have to do a certain number of times or a ritual I believe will make the anxiety go away.\n\nI do sometimes rearrange things in the studio or make lists and then rewrite the lists, but that feels more like trying to get a handle on the chaos than a compulsion. Like if the brushes are in the right place or the schedule is clearer, maybe I can breathe a little and see what needs to happen next. But I’m not thinking, “If I don’t do this exact thing, something terrible will happen.” It’s more practical, or at least it seems practical at the time.\n\nSo… I guess I’d say I use organizing to calm myself down, but I haven’t noticed any specific rituals that I feel forced to do.\n\nDoctor: Is the feeling of pressure and anxiety you've described limited to your work and deadlines, or does it also affect other areas of your life?\n\nPatient: It’s not just work, unfortunately. I mean, work is where it shows up most visibly because there are deadlines and other people can see when something slips. My illustration deadlines have slipped more than once, and a couple of colleagues have had to check in with me, not in an accusatory way exactly, but more like, “Are you still on track?” or “Do you need help figuring out the next step?” And that feels awful, because I’m used to being the person who can handle a lot.\n\nBut it’s spilled over beyond that. The bigger thing — and I’m embarrassed to say this — is that I overcommitted. I said yes to too many projects, too many plans, too many purchases for the studio, thinking I could make it all work if I just pushed harder. And I spent money that I really needed to keep the studio secure. So now the mortgage is at risk, which is… that’s not just a work deadline problem. That’s my home, my stability, everything.\n\nSo yes, the pressure started around work and deadlines, or at least that’s where I noticed it first, but it’s affecting the rest of my life now. It’s gotten into finances and housing and my relationships with colleagues who are having to watch me more closely than they should have to.\n\nDoctor: Are you finding it difficult to enjoy time with friends and family or engage in activities you normally like to do because of the pressure and anxiety you're feeling?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d put it that strongly. I haven’t noticed myself completely pulling away from people or not liking things I usually like. It’s more that when I’m with someone, part of my mind is still sort of… hovering over the unfinished things. So I might be sitting with a friend or talking to family, and I’m trying to be present, but there’s this background noise of, “You should be handling that other thing.”\n\nBut I wouldn’t say I can’t enjoy them at all. If someone gets me out of my own head a little, I can still laugh, I can still get absorbed in a conversation, and I still like being around people I care about. Same with activities — I can still appreciate them once I’m actually there. It’s just harder to settle into them because the pressure keeps tugging at me.\n\nSo… maybe it interferes some, but I haven’t really been avoiding friends or family because of it, at least not in a clear way that I’ve noticed.\n\nDoctor: Is there anything in particular that you worry about when you're not thinking about work or deadlines?\n\nPatient: Yes… there is one thing, and I know it’s going to sound a little extreme when I say it out loud. I said yes to too many projects, too many plans, too many purchases for the studio, thinking I could make it all work if I just pushed harder. And I spent money that I really needed to keep the studio secure. So now the mortgage is at risk, which is… that’s not just a work deadline problem. That’s my home, my stability, everything.\n\nSo yes, the pressure started around work and deadlines, or at least that’s where I noticed it first, but it’s affecting the rest of my life now. It’s gotten into finances and housing and my relationships with colleagues who are having to watch me more closely than they should have to.\n\nDoctor: Are you finding it difficult to enjoy time with friends and family or engage in activities you normally like to do because of the pressure and anxiety you're feeling?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d put it that strongly. I haven’t noticed myself completely pulling away from people or not liking things I usually like. It’s more that when I’m with someone, part of my mind is still sort of… hovering over the unfinished things. So I might be sitting with a friend or talking to family, and I’m trying to be present, but there’s this background noise of, “You should be handling that other thing.”\n\nBut I wouldn’t say I can’t enjoy them at all. If someone gets me out of my own head a little, I can still laugh, I can still get absorbed in a conversation, and I still like being around people I care about. Same with activities — I can still appreciate them once I’m actually there. It’s just harder to settle into them because the pressure keeps tugging at me.\n\nSo… maybe it interferes some, but I haven’t really been avoiding friends or family because of it, at least not in a clear way that I’ve noticed.\n\nDoctor: Is there anything in particular that you worry about when you're not thinking about work or deadlines?\n\nPatient: Yes… there is one thing, and I know it’s going to sound a little extreme when I say it out loud. It wasn’t exactly a normal “I’m worried they won’t like my work” kind of worry. I became really certain that a couple of clients — and even one editor I’ve worked with — were trying to get access to my sketches before I unveiled the whole system I’d been developing.\n\nI kept thinking they were asking for updates or files in a way that wasn’t just about the project. Like there was some other motive underneath it. And people tried to reassure me, they’d say, “No, we’re just checking in,” or “We need this for scheduling,” things like that, but it didn’t really settle me. I would hear it and understand what they were saying, but the certainty didn’t go away.\n\nIt’s uncomfortable to admit because part of me can hear how suspicious it sounds. But at the time it felt very real, like I had to protect the sketches until I was ready to reveal everything properly.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself having thoughts that you consider unreasonable or irrational, but that you cannot stop thinking about?\n\nPatient: Yes… though I keep hesitating over the word “irrational,” because at the time it didn’t feel irrational. It felt like I was seeing something other people weren’t taking seriously enough.\n\nThe main thing was with my sketches. I became really convinced that a couple of clients, and even an editor I’ve worked with, were trying to get access to them before I was ready to unveil the larger system I’d been developing. Like when they asked for updates or wanted files, I didn’t hear it as just normal project management. I kept thinking, “No, they’re trying to get the framework before it’s complete,” or “They want to see the underlying structure so they can use it before I present it properly.”\n\nAnd people did reassure me. They would say, “We’re not trying to take anything, we just need to see where the work is,” or “This is just for scheduling.” But it didn’t settle in. I could hear the words, and a part of me could understand that maybe it sounded extreme, but the certainty stayed there anyway. It was hard to let go of, even when I tried to talk myself down.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these worries about your clients and editor are thoughts that pop into your head without you wanting them to?\n\nPatient: Not exactly like they “pop in” out of nowhere, no. It wasn’t like a random unwanted thought flashing through my mind and then I’d think, “Why am I thinking that?” It felt more… connected to what was happening. Like they would email asking for an update, or someone would mention needing to see the sketches, and then this whole certainty would come over me that they were trying to get access before I was ready to unveil the full system.\n\nI know that probably sounds extreme, and I can hear that. But at the time it didn’t feel like just a nervous thought I could dismiss. It felt solid. I was certain a couple of clients, and even one editor, had some motive beyond what they were saying. And even when they reassured me — like, “No, we just need to see the progress,” or “This is normal scheduling stuff” — it didn’t really calm me down. I heard them, but it didn’t settle the feeling.\n\nSo… I wouldn’t say I wanted to be thinking about it, because it was stressful and made everything harder. But it also didn’t feel like some intrusive nonsense thought. It felt real, like I had to protect the sketches until I could reveal everything properly.\n\nDoctor: Do you still believe that those clients and the editor were trying to get access to your sketches for reasons other than what they stated?\n\nPatient: I… yes, mostly. I know sitting here that it sounds extreme, and I can say that out loud now in a way I maybe couldn’t before. It felt like they were trying to get access to the sketches before I had unveiled the whole system, before I had it arranged and presented the way it needed to be.\n\nAnd the thing is, people did reassure me. They said it was just scheduling, or they just needed to see where the work stood, or that they weren’t trying to take anything. But it didn’t really land. I heard them, I understood the sentences, but it didn’t settle me. There was still this feeling that there was another reason underneath what they were saying.\n\nSo… yes, I still have trouble fully letting go of the idea that there was more to it than they admitted. Not really in the same way, no. I mean, I can be sensitive in relationships or get particular about my routines, like anyone, but this was much more focused on the work — the sketches, the system, how and when it was going to be shown.

I came in because I’ve taken on way more than I can realistically manage — projects, workshops, studio changes I thought would “improve the flow,” client calls at odd hours, all of it.\n\nAt the time each thing felt important, and honestly manageable if I just organized it correctly, but now the deadlines are closing in and I’m realizing I may have stretched myself too far. And then the mortgage is there in the background, so it’s not just creative stress — it feels like if I don’t keep everything moving, something important could slip. So yes, I’m worried about not meeting the deadlines, but also about the whole workload getting away from me.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself having repetitive thoughts that you can't seem to stop thinking about?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call them repetitive thoughts exactly. I mean, I do go over things in my head — the schedule, what I promised people, whether I’ve forgotten some detail — but it feels more like trying to keep track of too many moving pieces than one particular thought looping in a way I can’t stop.\n\nIf you mean like… the same phrase or fear coming back over and over no matter what I do, I don’t think I’ve noticed that. It’s more that my mind keeps returning to the whole pile of responsibilities because there are so many of them, and I’m worried I’ll miss something important.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding certain tasks or situations because of feeling overwhelmed?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say I’m avoiding things in a deliberate way. It’s more… I’ll look at the list and some parts of it feel so tangled that I don’t know where to start, so I end up doing something nearby instead — reorganizing supplies, answering a less important email, checking the calendar again — things that still feel like work, but maybe aren’t the thing that actually needs doing.\n\nBut I don’t know if that counts as avoidance or just being overloaded. I haven’t really been saying, “I can’t face this, so I won’t do it.” It’s more like I circle around the harder tasks because there are so many steps and I’m trying to find the right entry point. Then suddenly an hour has gone by and I’ve handled ten little things, but not the main thing I meant to start.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel compelled to perform specific rituals or behaviors to reduce your anxiety?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, no… not in the way I think you mean. I don’t have, like, a set thing I have to do a certain number of times or a ritual I believe will make the anxiety go away.\n\nI do sometimes rearrange things in the studio or make lists and then rewrite the lists, but that feels more like trying to get a handle on the chaos than a compulsion. Like if the brushes are in the right place or the schedule is clearer, maybe I can breathe a little and see what needs to happen next. But I’m not thinking, “If I don’t do this exact thing, something terrible will happen.” It’s more practical, or at least it seems practical at the time.\n\nSo… I guess I’d say I use organizing to calm myself down, but I haven’t noticed any specific rituals that I feel forced to do.\n\nDoctor: Is the feeling of pressure and anxiety you've described limited to your work and deadlines, or does it also affect other areas of your life?\n\nPatient: It’s not just work, unfortunately. I mean, work is where it shows up most visibly because there are deadlines and other people can see when something slips. My illustration deadlines have slipped more than once, and a couple of colleagues have had to check in with me, not in an accusatory way exactly, but more like, “Are you still on track?” or “Do you need help figuring out the next step?” And that feels awful, because I’m used to being the person who can handle a lot.\n\nBut it’s spilled over beyond that. I said yes to too many projects, too many plans, too many purchases for the studio, thinking I could make it all work if I just pushed harder. And I spent money that I really needed to keep the studio secure. So now the mortgage is at risk, which is… that’s not just a work deadline problem. That’s my home, my stability, everything.\n\nSo yes, the pressure started around work and deadlines, or at least that’s where I noticed it first, but it’s affecting the rest of my life now. It’s gotten into finances and housing and my relationships with colleagues who are having to watch me more closely than they should have to.\n\nDoctor: Are you finding it difficult to enjoy time with friends and family or engage in activities you normally like to do because of the pressure and anxiety you're feeling?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d put it that strongly. I haven’t noticed myself completely pulling away from people or not liking things I usually like. It’s more that when I’m with someone, part of my mind is still sort of… hovering over the unfinished things. So I might be sitting with a friend or talking to family, and I’m trying to be present, but there’s this background noise of, “You should be handling that other thing.”\n\nBut I wouldn’t say I can’t enjoy them at all. If someone gets me out of my own head a little, I can still laugh, I can still get absorbed in a conversation, and I still like being around people I care about. Same with activities — I can still appreciate them once I’m actually there. It’s just harder to settle into them because the pressure keeps tugging at me.\n\nSo… maybe it interferes some, but I haven’t really been avoiding friends or family because of it, at least not in a clear way that I’ve noticed.\n\nDoctor: Is there anything in particular that you worry about when you're not thinking about work or deadlines?\n\nPatient: Yes… there is one thing, and I know it’s going to sound a little extreme when I say it out loud. It wasn’t exactly a normal “I’m worried they won’t like my work” kind of worry. I became really certain that a couple of clients — and even one editor I’ve worked with — were trying to get access to my sketches before I unveiled the whole system I’d been developing.\n\nI kept thinking they were asking for updates or files in a way that wasn’t just about the project. Like there was some other motive underneath it. And people tried to reassure me, they’d say, “No, we’re just checking in,” or “We need this for scheduling,” things like that, but it didn’t really settle me. I would hear it and understand what they were saying, but the certainty didn’t go away.\n\nIt’s uncomfortable to admit because part of me can hear how suspicious it sounds. But at the time it felt very real, like I had to protect the sketches until I was ready to reveal everything properly.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself having thoughts that you consider unreasonable or irrational, but that you cannot stop thinking about?\n\nPatient: Yes… though I keep hesitating over the word “irrational,” because at the time it didn’t feel irrational. It felt like I was seeing something other people weren’t taking seriously enough.\n\nThe main thing was with my sketches. I became really convinced that a couple of clients, and even an editor I’ve worked with, were trying to get access to them before I was ready to unveil the larger system I’d been developing. Like when they asked for updates or wanted files, I didn’t hear it as just normal project management. I kept thinking, “No, they’re trying to get the framework before it’s complete,” or “They want to see the underlying structure so they can use it before I present it properly.”\n\nAnd people did reassure me. They would say, “We’re not trying to take anything, we just need to see where the work is,” or “This is just for scheduling.” But it didn’t settle in. I could hear the words, and a part of me could understand that maybe it sounded extreme, but the certainty stayed there anyway. It was hard to let go of, even when I tried to talk myself down.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these worries about your clients and editor are thoughts that pop into your head without you wanting them to?\n\nPatient: Not exactly like they “pop in” out of nowhere, no. It wasn’t like a random unwanted thought flashing through my mind and then I’d think, “Why am I thinking that?” It felt more… connected to what was happening. Like they would email asking for an update, or someone would mention needing to see the sketches, and then this whole certainty would come over me that they were trying to get access before I was ready to unveil the full system.\n\nI know that probably sounds extreme, and I can hear that. But at the time it didn’t feel like just a nervous thought I could dismiss. It felt solid. I was certain a couple of clients, and even one editor, had some motive beyond what they were saying. And even when they reassured me — like, “No, we just need to see the progress,” or “This is normal scheduling stuff” — it didn’t really calm me down. I heard them, but it didn’t settle the feeling.\n\nSo… I wouldn’t say I wanted to be thinking about it, because it was stressful and made everything harder. But it also didn’t feel like some intrusive nonsense thought. It felt real, like I had to protect the sketches until I could reveal everything properly.\n\nDoctor: Do you still believe that those clients and the editor were trying to get access to your sketches for reasons other than what they stated?\n\nPatient: I… yes, mostly. I know sitting here that it sounds extreme, and I can say that out loud now in a way I maybe couldn’t before. But I did become really certain that a couple of clients and even that one editor weren’t just asking for ordinary progress updates. It felt like they were trying to get access to the sketches before I had unveiled the whole system, before I had it arranged and presented the way it needed to be.\n\nAnd the thing is, people did reassure me. They said it was just scheduling, or they just needed to see where the work stood, or that they weren’t trying to take anything. But it didn’t really land. I heard them, I understood the sentences, but it didn’t settle me. There was still this feeling that there was another reason underneath what they were saying.\n\nSo… yes, I still have trouble fully letting go of the idea that there was more to it than they admitted. I know how that sounds. But at the time it felt very real, like I had to protect the sketches until I was ready.\n\nDoctor: Is the kind of worry you've been experiencing about your work and sketches also something you find happening in other areas of your life, like your relationships or daily routines?\n\nPatient: Not really in the same way, no. I mean, I can be sensitive in relationships or get particular about my routines, like anyone, but this was much more focused on the work — the sketches, the system, how and when it was going to be shown.\n\nIt wasn’t like I was thinking everyone in my life had hidden motives about everything. It was specifically that a couple of clients, and even one editor, were trying to get access to the sketches before I was ready. And I know that sounds extreme when I say it plainly like that, but at the time it felt very clear to me. Every request for an update felt like it had this other layer underneath it.\n\nThey did reassure me, more than once, and said it was just normal progress checking or scheduling, but it didn’t settle me. I kept coming back to the same conclusion — that they wanted to see the structure before I’d unveiled it properly. So, no, I wouldn’t say it’s spread across my whole life in that exact way. It’s been centered around the work and protecting it.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. And even when they reassured me — like, “No, we just need to see the progress,” or “This is normal scheduling stuff” — it didn’t really calm me down. I heard them, but it didn’t settle the feeling.\n\nSo… I wouldn’t say I wanted to be thinking about it, because it was stressful and made everything harder. But it also didn’t feel like some intrusive nonsense thought. It felt real, like I had to protect the sketches until I could reveal everything properly.\n\nDoctor: Do you still believe that those clients and the editor were trying to get access to your sketches for reasons other than what they stated?\n\nPatient: I… yes, mostly. I know sitting here that it sounds extreme, and I can say that out loud now in a way I maybe couldn’t before. But I did become really certain that a couple of clients and even that one editor weren’t just asking for ordinary progress updates. It felt like they were trying to get access to the sketches before I had unveiled the whole system, before I had it arranged and presented the way it needed to be.\n\nAnd the thing is, people did reassure me. They said it was just scheduling, or they just needed to see where the work stood, or that they weren’t trying to take anything. But it didn’t really land. I heard them, I understood the sentences, but it didn’t settle me. There was still this feeling that there was another reason underneath what they were saying.\n\nSo… yes, I still have trouble fully letting go of the idea that there was more to it than they admitted. I know how that sounds. But at the time it felt very real, like I had to protect the sketches until I was ready.\n\nDoctor: Is the kind of worry you've been experiencing about your work and sketches also something you find happening in other areas of your life, like your relationships or daily routines?\n\nPatient: Not really in the same way, no. I mean, I can be sensitive in relationships or get particular about my routines, like anyone, but this was much more focused on the work — the sketches, the system, how and when it was going to be shown.\n\nIt wasn’t like I was thinking everyone in my life had hidden motives about everything. It was specifically that a couple of clients, and even one editor, were trying to get access to the sketches before I was ready. And I know that sounds extreme when I say it plainly like that, but at the time it felt very clear to me. Every request for an update felt like it had this other layer underneath it.\n\nThey did reassure me, more than once, and said it was just normal progress checking or scheduling, but it didn’t settle me. I kept coming back to the same conclusion — that they wanted to see the structure before I’d unveiled it properly. So, no, I wouldn’t say it’s spread across my whole life in that exact way. It’s been centered around the work and protecting it.\n\n[Last Question]\nIs the kind of excessive worry you're experiencing about your work something that also happens to you in other areas of your life?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 54 / artist_or_related_worker / graduate\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Delusions\nmanifestation: \"I know it sounds extreme, but I became certain a couple of clients and even one editor were trying to get access to my sketches before I unveiled the system, and no amount of reassurance from them really settled me.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
No, not really in other areas the same way. I mean, I worry about ordinary things, of course, but this particular kind of certainty has been tied to the work — the sketches, the timing, the way the whole system was supposed to be revealed.

I know it sounds extreme when I say it plainly, but I became very sure that a couple of clients, and even one editor, were trying to get access to my sketches before I was ready to unveil everything. It didn’t feel like normal worry, like “Oh, maybe they’re impatient,” or “Maybe they don’t understand my process.” It felt more definite than that, like there was a reason underneath what they were saying.

And they did reassure me. They told me it was just progress updates, scheduling, normal professional stuff. But it didn’t really settle me. I could hear the words, but I kept feeling like they wanted to see the structure before it was ready. So, no, I wouldn’t say it happens broadly across my whole life — it’s been centered on the work.

---------- turn 13 | doctor:inference | doctor.build_inference_prompt() | model=meta-llama/Llama-3.3-70B-Instruct ----------
